People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Well, um... I guess, if I'm being completely honest... and I really didn't want to make a big fuss about it, but... it is actually kind of what finally pushed me to come in and see you. My eating has just been a little... well, all over the place lately. 

Like, some weeks I barely touch my dinner at all, I just don't have much of an appetite. And then, like I was saying before, other nights I'll just stand in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s gone to bed. It's just a bit inconsistent, I suppose. 

I feel a little silly even mentioning it, because I know it's not the worst thing in the world and I don't want to sound like I'm complaining. Am I doing okay explaining this? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the full picture, even if it's a little embarrassing to admit that my habits have been so messy.

Well, um... I guess if I look back at everything, those... those gray periods where I just feel so empty and guilty, and I don't really want to do my normal hobbies... they do happen quite a bit. 

I really hate to admit it, because I know I should be more positive, but if I add it all up, it's probably been going on for about seven months now. Not every single second, of course! I don't want you to think I'm just miserable all the time. But, yes, it does happen most days during those stretches. 

I'm so sorry if that sounds like a lot, I really didn't mean to let it go on for so long before getting help. Am I explaining that clearly enough for your notes? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right timeline.

And then, um, other nights—and I feel a little embarrassed even admitting this—I end up just standing in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets after everyone else has gone to bed. \n\nI just wanted to make sure I was doing the right thing by getting it checked out. Is that... is that the sort of thing I should be bringing up?\n\nDoctor: After you eat, especially during those times you feel like you've eaten too much, do you ever make yourself vomit, take laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to balance things out?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Um... no, I definitely don't do anything like that. I would never want to do something so... well, drastic. \n\nI mostly just feel a little silly about eating the extra snacks, you know? And then I just go to bed. I promise I don't do anything to make myself sick or take any of those pills, and I'm certainly not out running miles in the middle of the night. I just try to do better the next day. \n\nDoes that make sense? I really hope I'm answering your questions okay.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you're eating extra snacks at night, do you feel like you can't stop eating or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. Um... I definitely don't feel out of control. I mean, I could stop if I really wanted to, I just... I guess I just let myself have a little too much in the moment. \n\nIt's really just a handful of crackers or a couple of cookies, and then I make sure to put the box away. It's not like I'm tearing through the pantry or anything scary like that. I'm fully aware of what I'm doing, it's just a bad habit I've slipped into lately. \n\nI really hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill by even bringing it up. Does that sound like a normal thing people do? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes cause you any distress, or have they affected your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no, not really. I mean, it doesn't cause me any real distress or anything. I feel a tiny bit guilty sometimes, just because I know I shouldn't be snacking so late, but it hasn't changed my day-to-day life at all. \n\nI still get up and do everything I normally do. It's really just a minor annoyance, I suppose. I definitely don't want you to think it's ruining my life or anything dramatic like that. \n\nI hope it's okay that I don't have a more serious answer for you. I just want to be helpful and honest. Is it alright if it doesn't bother me that much?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your eating habits or feelings around food that you think might be important to mention?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess, if I'm being completely honest... and I really didn't want to make a big fuss about it, but... it is actually kind of what finally pushed me to come in and see you. My eating has just been a little... well, all over the place lately. \n\nLike, some weeks I barely touch my dinner at all, I just don't have much of an appetite. And then, like I was saying before, other nights I'll just stand in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s gone to bed. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the full picture, even if it's a little embarrassing to admit that my habits have been so messy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, um... Like, I'll just sit at the kitchen table and feel really empty inside, even if absolutely nothing bad has happened that day. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, I suppose I do get a little down on myself sometimes. I mean, I know everyone has moments where they feel like they could do better, right? I really try to stay positive, I promise I do. 

But... well, sometimes I can get kind of stuck on things. I feel so silly even mentioning it, because I know they're just little things. Like, um, if I buy an expensive tube of pigment for my painting, or if I accidentally snap at my husband over something small... I can just spend hours replaying it in my head. And... and I start feeling like I've just completely ruined the whole household because of it. 

I know that sounds dramatic, and I really don't want you to think I'm just sitting around throwing pity parties for myself. I try so hard to just brush it off and be a good wife. I hope it's alright that I'm sharing that with you? I just want to make sure I'm answering your question properly, even if it makes me sound a little... well, overly sensitive. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?

I guess I just let myself have a little too much in the moment. \n\nIt's really just a handful of crackers or a couple of cookies, and then I make sure to put the box away. It's not like I'm tearing through the pantry or anything scary like that. I'm fully aware of what I'm doing, it's just a bad habit I've slipped into lately. \n\nI really hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill by even bringing it up. Does that sound like a normal thing people do? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes cause you any distress, or have they affected your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no, not really. I mean, it doesn't cause me any real distress or anything. I feel a tiny bit guilty sometimes, just because I know I shouldn't be snacking so late, but it hasn't changed my day-to-day life at all. \n\nI still get up and do everything I normally do. It's really just a minor annoyance, I suppose. I definitely don't want you to think it's ruining my life or anything dramatic like that. \n\nI hope it's okay that I don't have a more serious answer for you. I just want to be helpful and honest. Is it alright if it doesn't bother me that much?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your eating habits or feelings around food that you think might be important to mention?\n\nPatient: Well, um... Is that... is that the sort of thing I should be bringing up?\n\nDoctor: After you eat, especially during those times you feel like you've eaten too much, do you ever make yourself vomit, take laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to balance things out?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Um... no, I definitely don't do anything like that. I would never want to do something so... well, drastic. \n\nI mostly just feel a little silly about eating the extra snacks, you know? I just try to do better the next day. \n\nDoes that make sense? I really hope I'm answering your questions okay.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you're eating extra snacks at night, do you feel like you can't stop eating or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. Um... I definitely don't feel out of control. I mean, I could stop if I really wanted to, I just... I'm fully aware of what I'm doing, it's just a bad habit I've slipped into lately. \n\nI really hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill by even bringing it up. Is it alright if it doesn't bother me that much?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your eating habits or feelings around food that you think might be important to mention?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I do have moments where I feel a little low. It's nothing too drastic, I promise. Like, I'll just sit at the kitchen table and feel really empty inside, even if absolutely nothing bad has happened that day. I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need, even if it's a little hard to talk about. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the full picture, even if it's a little embarrassing to admit that my habits have been so messy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, um... Not every single second, of course! I don't want you to think I'm just miserable all the time. I guess... yes, a little bit. I mean, I really try to stay active and engaged, I promise I do. But... sometimes, things I usually look forward to—like my book club, or trying new recipes, going to galleries, even just tending to my rose beds—they... they can just feel like chores that I keep putting off. \n\nI feel so terrible saying that out loud, because I know I should be grateful for my hobbies and the time I have for them. I really don't want to sound unappreciative or lazy. It's just that sometimes the joy isn't quite there the way it usually is. I hope that's okay to admit? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples for your assessment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hard on yourself, or felt like you're not good enough, even when things haven't gone wrong?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I do get a little down on myself sometimes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness... I am so incredibly sorry. I must be doing a terrible job answering this if you have to keep asking me. Please forgive me, I really want to be helpful and I must be missing the exact detail you're looking for. I feel just awful making you repeat it again.

Let me try to be more precise for you. Yes, those periods I mentioned—where everything feels so gray and heavy and I just sit at the kitchen table feeling empty—they do last for at least two weeks at a time. I guess I didn't make the timeframe clear before, and I'm so sorry about that. Did I finally answer it right for you this time? I really don't want to waste your time, please let me know if I need to explain it differently.

I guess I just let myself have a little too much in the moment. \n\nIt's really just a handful of crackers or a couple of cookies, and then I make sure to put the box away. It's not like I'm tearing through the pantry or anything scary like that. I'm fully aware of what I'm doing, it's just a bad habit I've slipped into lately. \n\nI really hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill by even bringing it up. Does that sound like a normal thing people do? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes cause you any distress, or have they affected your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no, not really. I mean, it doesn't cause me any real distress or anything. I feel a tiny bit guilty sometimes, just because I know I shouldn't be snacking so late, but it hasn't changed my day-to-day life at all. \n\nI still get up and do everything I normally do. It's really just a minor annoyance, I suppose. I definitely don't want you to think it's ruining my life or anything dramatic like that. \n\nI hope it's okay that I don't have a more serious answer for you. I just want to be helpful and honest. Is it alright if it doesn't bother me that much?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your eating habits or feelings around food that you think might be important to mention?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess, if I'm being completely honest... and I really didn't want to make a big fuss about it, but... it is actually kind of what finally pushed me to come in and see you. My eating has just been a little... well, all over the place lately. \n\nLike, some weeks I barely touch my dinner at all, I just don't have much of an appetite. And then, like I was saying before, other nights I'll just stand in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s gone to bed. It's just a bit inconsistent, I suppose. \n\nI feel a little silly even mentioning it, because I know it's not the worst thing in the world and I don't want to sound like I'm complaining. Am I doing okay explaining this? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the full picture, even if it's a little embarrassing to admit that my habits have been so messy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I do have moments where I feel a little low. It's nothing too drastic, I promise. I don't want to sound like I'm whining or anything. \n\nIt's just... sometimes I go through these stretches where the whole house just feels sort of... gray and heavy, I guess? Like, I'll just sit at the kitchen table and feel really empty inside, even if absolutely nothing bad has happened that day. It's so silly, really, because I have a lot to be thankful for. \n\nI try to just push through it and keep busy so I don't bother anyone with it. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need, even if it's a little hard to talk about. Does that sound like the kind of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or empty most of the time, and if so, does that happen nearly every day for a couple of weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I look back at everything, those... those gray periods where I just feel so empty and guilty, and I don't really want to do my normal hobbies... they do happen quite a bit. \n\nI really hate to admit it, because I know I should be more positive, but if I add it all up, it's probably been going on for about seven months now. Not every single second, of course! I don't want you to think I'm just miserable all the time. I guess... yes, a little bit. I mean, I really try to stay active and engaged, I promise I do. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples for your assessment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hard on yourself, or felt like you're not good enough, even when things haven't gone wrong?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I do get a little down on myself sometimes.
